<meta>This page serves as the title page for a 1907 edition of the 'Muhurtachintamani', a foundational Sanskrit text on electional astrology. It identifies the commentator, the famous publisher Khemraj Shrikrishnadas, and the legal copyright status under British Indian law.</meta>

## ## Prosperity! <note>original: "Shree," a traditional invocation to the divine to ensure the successful completion of a task</note>

# The Wish-Fulfilling Gem of Auspicious Moments
<note>original: "Muhurtachintamani." A <term>Muhurta</term> is a unit of time or an "auspicious moment" selected for performing rituals, weddings, or business. This 16th-century text by Rama Daivajna is one of the most famous guides for choosing these times.</note>

---

->Bombay<-

--- Page 4 ---
<meta>Archive: Shri Krishna Museum, Kurukshetra; Digitized by eGangotri. This page introduces the foundational importance of Jyotisha (astronomy/astrology) within the Vedic tradition.</meta>

# Preface

---

->The science of Jyotisha, composed of the three branches of Mathematical Astronomy, Natural Astrology, and Horoscopy,<-
->Is the pure and stainless eye of the Veda. || 1 ||<-
->Other sciences are not directly perceivable; in them, there is only dispute.<-
->The science of Jyotisha is directly perceptible, where the Moon and Sun are the witnesses. || 2 ||<-
->Without this, all Vedic and traditional religious duties cannot be accomplished.<-
->Therefore, for the welfare of the world, this was created by Brahma in ancient times. || 3 ||<-

The <term>Veda</term> <note>The oldest and most sacred scriptures of Hinduism</note> has six limbs: Phonetics, Ritual, Grammar, Etymology, Prosody, and Astrology (*Jyotisha*). Among these, the most excellent limb—designated as the "eye"—is the pure and stainless science of Astrology. Ancient sages revealed this in three "branches" (*skandhas*): **Mathematical Astronomy** (*Siddhanta*), **Compendiums on natural omens and timing** (*Samhita*), and **Horoscopy** (*Hora*), which includes natal charts and specialized systems like <term>Tajik</term> <note>A system of Persian-influenced annual solar return horoscopy</note>. Without this science, all <term>Shrauta-Smarta</term> <note>Vedic rituals and traditional social/religious duties based on the Law-codes</note> actions cannot be successfully completed.

Therefore, for the benefit of the world, Lord Brahma described it as the "eye of the Veda." For this reason, the "twice-born" <note>original: *dvija*; refers to those initiated into Vedic study—historically the Brahmin, Kshatriya, and Vaishya communities</note> are commanded to study this science with great effort. In other sciences, there is much dispute, and the results are not as clearly visible as they are in the directly observable and wondrous science of Astrology. The proofs of this are seen in the Sun and the Moon, the rising and setting of stars, and the waxing and waning of the moon's crescent. 

In the texts on <term>Shiksha</term> <note>Vedic Phonetics</note>, it is also written: *"Phonetics is the nose of the Veda, Grammar is the mouth. Astrology is called the eye, and Etymology is the ear. Prosody represents the feet, and the Ritual codes are the hands."* Even if all other limbs and parts of the body are complete, just as everything remains in darkness without eyes, so too are all religious instruments rendered useless without this science. 

The *Vashistha Siddhanta* <note>An ancient astronomical treatise attributed to the sage Vashistha</note> also contains the sentence: *"This science is indeed the eye of the Veda; it holds primacy among the limbs because of the wealth of its meaning. A man may be complete in his body with all other limbs, but without eyes, he is nothing."* 

Despite these many authoritative proofs, in the present day, many contemporary gentlemen often say that Astrology is of no substance. They claim that in the past, only the Brahmins were learned, and being wise, they made this far-sighted calculation: "If our descendants become lacking in knowledge or prowess, how will they maintain their livelihood?"

<note>The vocabulary section below defines the key technical terms used in the Sanskrit and Hindi text above.</note>
**Vocabulary:** <term>Jyotisha</term> (Astrology/Astronomy), <term>Siddhanta</term> (Mathematical Astronomy), <term>Samhita</term> (Natural/Social Astrology), <term>Hora</term> (Natal Horoscopy), <term>Veda</term> (Sacred Knowledge), <term>Vedanga</term> (Limbs of the Veda), <term>Shrauta-Smarta</term> (Vedic and Traditional Rituals).

<summary>This preface establishes Jyotisha (Astrology/Astronomy) as the "eye" of the Vedas, arguing that it is the only science providing direct visual proof (through the sun and moon) for timing sacred rituals. It defends the discipline against modern critics who dismiss it as a priestly invention for financial survival.</summary>
<keywords>Jyotisha, Vedanga, Astronomy, Astrology, Veda, Shrauta, Smarta, Brahma, Vashistha Siddhanta, Siddhanta, Samhita, Hora</keywords>

--- Page 5 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory arguments regarding the divine origin and practical necessity of astrology, establishing it as a foundational science for both spiritual and temporal life.</meta>

<page-num>( 4 )</page-num>
-># Preface<-

...will it do? For this reason, **Astrology** <note>original: "Jyotish-shastra"</note> was created so that it would be evident to everyone and they would respect the Brahmins. Although many engage in such debates and counter-arguments, one must ask: who originally created this science and when? It is common knowledge that <term>Astronomy</term> <note>original: "Khagola" - the study of the celestial sphere</note>, <term>Geography</term> <note>original: "Bhugola" - the study of the earth</note>, <term>Land Surveying</term> <note>original: "Bhumiman"</note>, the mathematics of Solar and Lunar eclipses, and the divisions of time such as day, night, fortnight, month, and year are all revealed through Astrology alone. As for the aspect of predicting results <note>original: "Phaladesh"</note>, it is the wisdom of the ancient founding teachers <note>original: "Acharyas"</note> that while it is evident that all living beings receive fruits according to their own past actions, those actions and their consequences are invisible. To make them visible, those great souls established specific calculations <note>original: "Hisab"</note> and assigned them names such as the Sun and other planets, lunar dates, days of the week, constellations, yogas, karanas, ascendants, and auspicious moments <note>original: "Muhurta"</note>. Through these, a thoughtful reader can describe the fruits of the past, future, and present. Just as in many mathematical operations a certain factor <note>original: "Karana"</note> is assumed to complete a task, similarly, in the predictive side of astrology, the "desired time" and "auspicious moment" serve as the factors through which all tasks are accomplished. Furthermore, this is a primary limb—the Eye—of the **Veda-Personified** <note>original: "Vedamurti" - referring to God or the personified Vedas</note>. The Veda validates this; without it, no rituals (sacrificial acts) of the revealed or traditional scriptures <note>original: "Shrauta-Smarta"</note> can be performed, and its miracles are directly visible.

<margin>1</margin>
> "Knowledge came to the Brahmin and said: 'Protect me, I am your treasure. Do not speak me to the scornful or the crooked, so that I may remain powerful.'" <note>A traditional Sanskrit verse emphasizing the sanctity of sacred knowledge.</note>

And so on. The primary importance of Astrology in this regard is as follows: (Verse)

> "Other sciences are merely for entertainment; there is nothing particularly special about them. But Medicine, Astrology, and the Science of Mantras bring conviction at every single step."

Other sciences are merely for amusement (to please the heart or for entertainment). But the science of medicine, the science of astrology, the science of mantras, and the codes of dharma provide proof (faith) at every step. For instance, in astrology, there is the direct mathematics of the planets: the <term>elevation of the moon's horns</term> <note>original: "Shringonnati" - the visible tips of the crescent moon</note>, eclipses, <term>planetary conjunctions</term> <note>original: "Grahyuti"</note>, and the shadows of the planets measured by quadrants or sighting tubes—these all align perfectly at the correct time. Furthermore, in the consideration of births, yearly charts, and queries, if the "desired time" is accurate and the practitioner is well-learned, the results for the past, future, and present are found to be exactly correct. For the welfare of the world, Lord Brahma established this among the "limbs" of the Vedas after the division of the Vedas.

<margin>2</margin>
> "A Brahmin should be initiated in his eighth year; one should perform the New and Full Moon sacrifices."

Such is the revelation <note>original: "Shruti"</note>. The calculation of the "eighth year" is based solely on mathematics according to the movement of the Sun, and the knowledge of the <term>New and Full Moon sacrifices</term> <note>original: "Darsha-Paurnamasa"</note> cannot exist without Astrology. It is also written:

> "The Vedas are indeed intended for the purpose of sacrifice, and sacrifices are ordained according to the sequence of time. Therefore, he who knows this Science of Time-Keeping—Astrology—knows the sacrifices."

Sacrifice <note>original: "Yajna"</note> is God Himself; the Vedas are useful for this; the measure of time belongs to time, and since the Supreme Soul is the "Soul of Time," the Sacrificial Being is called the "Soul of Time." That indeed is Astrology, by which...

<summary>This page argues that astrology is the "Eye of the Vedas," essential for performing religious rituals and understanding the invisible consequences of karma through visible planetary movements. It asserts that unlike theoretical sciences, astrology provides empirical proof through astronomical events and accurate predictions.</summary>
<keywords>Astrology, Jyotish, Vedas, Karma, Astronomy, Time-keeping, Sacrificial Rituals, Brahma, Mathematics</keywords>

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>This page serves as the formal introduction to a treatise on electional astrology (Muhurta). It establishes the royal patronage of the Kings of Garhwal and the translator's intent to make complex Sanskrit astronomical knowledge accessible to a broader public through vernacular commentary.</meta>

<page-num>5</page-num>
<header>Preface.</header>

# Preface.

Without the knowledge of time, nothing can be known; without the knowledge of time, sacrifices <note>Yajnas: Vedic rituals or offerings</note> and other sacred acts cannot be performed. There are many mutual proofs of this. However, setting aside lengthy explanations for now, the primary purpose of writing this is to address a specific need: although the science of Astrology <note>Jyotish-shastra: often called the "Eye of the Veda"</note> is unique and provides direct, miraculous evidence, it does not suddenly blossom in the heart-lotuses of the general public. On the contrary, a false impression of the science begins to arise naturally due to the passage of time. The reason for this is the decline of the original language (Sanskrit) due to the influence of the current age. For this reason, this "direct science" <note>Pratyaksha Shastra: a science whose results can be observed, like eclipses or planetary movements</note> is gradually disappearing.

Secondly, in this time of limited acquaintance with Sanskrit, many people—having seen or heard a few general predictions, or having practiced some form of spirit-lore <note>Bhuta-vidya: occult practices or exorcism</note>—show off momentarily charming and miraculous things to enchant people's minds, profiting from their slight confusion. At that time, these charlatans are called "Pandits" <note>Scholars</note>, but in the end, the results they predicted prove to be untrustworthy. Because of this, a public rumor takes hold that "Astrology itself is a fraud." No one speaks of the cleverness of those charlatans.

In such a state of affairs, I realized that the only way to rescue the situation and make the general public well-versed in Astrology was to create vernacular commentaries <note>Bhasha-tika: translations or explanations in the common tongue, likely Hindi in this context</note> on the prevailing texts (whose meanings the general public cannot otherwise grasp). 

Therefore, according to the command given some time ago by the Great King of the Garhwal Kingdom, the Sun of the Kshatriya Clan, the living image of Lord Badri <note>Badrinath</note>, His Majesty King Pratap Shah Dev Bahadur; and currently by the command of his virtuous son, the Illustrious King Kirti Shah Dev Bahadur; I have already published vernacular commentaries on the following:
* From the three branches of astrology, specifically the branch of Horoscopy <note>Hora: predictive astrology based on birth charts</note>, the primary text *Brihat Jataka*.
* Among the Persian-influenced systems <note>Tajika: a branch of Indian astrology incorporating Persian techniques</note>, the primary three-part work *Nilakanthi*, including all considerations for inquiry <note>Prashna: horary astrology or answering specific questions</note>.
* The texts *Chamatkara Chintamani* and *Bhava Kutuhala*.

Now, I have decided to publish a vernacular commentary on a text concerning Auspicious Moments <note>Muhurta: electional astrology used to pick the best time for an event</note>, similar to a compendium or a specialized table. Knowledge of "Muhurta" is necessary for everyone in all types of work, and the result of a "Good Moment" is always auspicious. The major texts on this subject, such as the *Samhitas*, are very long to study, and those that are short have very limited utility. 

Therefore, I have chosen this text named **"Muhurta Chintamani"** (The Thought-Gem of Auspicious Moments). It is elegant in its reading, adorned with various poetic meters, and rich in meaning. It has a further special quality: in other works like *Ratnamala*, there are separate chapters for lunar days, weekdays, and constellations. To find a single auspicious moment for one task, one must look through many different sections. In this book, whatever task you wish to examine, you can find everything in one place—from the lunar day to the rising sign <note>Lagna</note> and its subdivisions, and even the decisions based on Religious Law <note>Dharmashastra</note>. Because of these excellent qualities, this modern text has become prevalent and is accepted as an authority everywhere. However, its meaning is not easily grasped, therefore...

<summary>The author introduces a vernacular commentary on the "Muhurta Chintamani," arguing that Sanskrit's decline has allowed charlatans to discredit astrology. He cites the patronage of the Kings of Garhwal (Pratap Shah and Kirti Shah) as the impetus for his work in making astrological timing accessible to the public.</summary>

<keywords>Astrology, Jyotish, Muhurta Chintamani, Garhwal Kingdom, King Pratap Shah, King Kirti Shah, Sanskrit decline, vernacular commentary, electional astrology, Nilakanthi, Brihat Jataka</keywords>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page concludes the introductory preface of the work, establishing the publisher's intent and the quality of the translation provided.</meta>

<page-num>6</page-num>
# Preface

I deemed it appropriate to create a vernacular commentary <note>original: "bhashatika"; a translation or explanation in the common language, likely Hindi in this context, to make Sanskrit technical terms accessible</note> on this work. By consulting this, even those who only possess a basic knowledge of the almanac <note>original: "panchang"; the traditional Hindu calendar used to track celestial movements</note> will be able to understand the determination of auspicious timing <term>Muhurta: the selection of the most beneficial time to begin an activity to ensure success</term> in an excellent manner. Furthermore, it will provide great ease for both teachers and students.

Although a vernacular commentary of this text has been printed previously, the reason for undertaking this effort once again will be understood by learned readers and wise scholars upon examining the essence of this commentary. They will see how a simple, clear, and fluent meaning has been revealed, strictly following the original intention of the Master Author. Thoughtful and virtuous people will, with great pleasure, find this labor—undertaken for the benefit of others—to be successful and meaningful.

->Khemraj Shrikrishnadas,<-
->Manager, "Shri Venkateshwar" Steam Press—Bombay.<-

<summary>The publisher Khemraj Shrikrishnadas introduces a new, simplified commentary on a technical Sanskrit text to make the calculation of auspicious timings accessible to general readers and students. He emphasizes that this new edition is clearer and more faithful to the original author's intent than previous versions.</summary>
<keywords>Khemraj Shrikrishnadas, Shri Venkateshwar Steam Press, Muhurta, Almanac, Astrology, Bombay printing history, Vernacular commentary</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page begins the translation of the Muhurta Chintamani, a seminal 16th-century Sanskrit text on electional astrology (choosing auspicious times). The translator, Mahidhara, provides a vernacular Hindi commentary to make the complex Sanskrit verses accessible to students.</meta>

->॥ Salutations to the Holy Lord Ganesha ॥<-

# Now begins the Muhurta Chintamani
## With its Vernacular Commentary

---

> Relying upon the long boat of the lotus feet of the Holy Lord <note>original: "Shrinath"; refers to the Supreme Teacher or Vishnu</note>, I have set out to cross the ocean of poetry created by the poet Rama Daivajna, which is impassable even for the learned. How small and insignificant am I! ॥ 1 ॥
> Having obtained knowledge of auspicious timing <term>Muhurta: a unit of time approximately 48 minutes long, or the general study of electional astrology</term> through the grace of my father’s lotus feet, I, named Mahidhara, extend this explanation in the language of men <note>Hindi</note> for the delight of the young <note>referring to students or beginners in the field</note>. Whatever faults are mine herein should be forgiven by the wise. ॥ 2 ॥

The author of the commentary, in order to remove obstacles, offers salutations to his Guru and states the purpose of this vernacular composition: The ocean of poetry by the virtuous poet Rama Daivajna is such that even scholars cannot cross it suddenly—meaning one cannot grasp the poet's intent without a foundation. Therefore, I, a small and humble soul, having found the great boat of the lotus feet of the Holy Lord (either Vishnu, the Lord of Lakshmi; or Shiva, the Primal Lord; or specifically the Supreme Brahman in the form of the Sat-Chit-Ananda Guru), have prepared to cross said ocean of poetry. I, named Mahidhara (a Brahmin residing in the capital of Tehri, Garhwal district), who has received the knowledge of Muhurta through the grace of my father's lotus feet, compose this fluid Hindi commentary on the text titled "Muhurta Chintamani" for the pleasure of those unfamiliar with such treatises. I also pray that if there be any unworthiness or error on my part, the learned should forgive me. ॥ 1 ॥ 2 ॥

->The Teacher first performs the auspicious invocation in the Indravajra meter:<-

> (Indravajra Meter) May the elephant-faced God <note>Ganesha</note> remove all obstacles—He who, having pulled the decorative Ketaki leaf-petal from Gauri’s <note>Parvati's</note> ear with His hand <note>trunk</note>, placed it before His mouth; for a moment <term>Muhurta</term>, it appeared as if a second tusk was sprouting forth. ॥ 1 ॥

Lord Ganesha, in a display of childhood playfulness toward His mother Gauri (Parvati), forcibly pulled a piece of the Ketaki flower petal worn in her ear with His trunk. He held it to one side of His mouth as if to eat it. For that brief moment (Muhurta), it looked as if a second tusk <note>Ganesha is traditionally "Ekadanta" or single-tusked</note> was growing...

<summary>This opening page contains the traditional invocations (Mangalacharana) by both the original 16th-century author Rama Daivajna and the commentator Mahidhara, establishing the text's focus on electional astrology.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the introductory invocations and begins the technical exposition of the Muhurta Chintamani, a classic text on electional astrology.</meta>

<page-num>2</page-num>
<header>The Jewel of Thought on Auspicious Moments</header>

<note>original: "Muhurta Chintamani"</note>

The beauty of the scene was observed because Lord Ganesha has a single tusk; however, by placing pieces of the Ketaki flower <note>Pandanus flower</note> there for a short time, he appeared as if he had two tusks. This is a wonderful poetic ornament of comparison. May that Ganesha—who is called "Elephant-faced" <note>original: "dvipasya"</note> because he drinks once with his trunk and again with his mouth—remove all obstacles. ॥ 1 ॥

<margin>red mark</margin> (Upajati Meter) To provide the means for understanding the entire collection of ritual actions, containing the play of essential meanings in a concise form—
->Rama, the son of the astrologer Ananta, extends this "Jewel of Thought on Auspicious Moments."<- ॥ 2 ॥

The "collection of actions" refers to the entire group of rituals beginning with birth ceremonies; the "means for understanding" refers to the cause for knowing whether a specific action is auspicious or inauspicious on a given day. This work contains the "play of essential meanings" within it, meaning it expresses the core conclusions in concise words. Although there are many ancient texts on auspicious moments <term>Muhurta: a unit of time approximately 48 minutes long, or more broadly, the study of choosing the right time for an event</term>, they contain vast readings and separate chapters for considering dates and other factors. In this book, the management of all tasks is accomplished in just a few words in a single place. Therefore, this book is a "Jewel" <note>Mani</note> for the "Thought" <note>Chinta</note>—the determination of auspiciousness and inauspiciousness—regarding the specific purity of the day or the fifteen divisions of the day. Just as a diamond is the foundation of all lustrous things, this book is the foundation for all auspicious timing. Rama the Astrologer <note>Rama Daivajna</note>, the son of the world-renowned astrologer named Ananta, expands upon this—that is, he describes the arrangement of prescriptions and prohibitions. ॥ 2 ॥

(Anushtup Meter) Fire, Brahma, Gauri, Ganesha, the Serpent, Guha, the Sun,
->Shiva, Durga, Yama, the All-gods, Vishnu, Cupid, Shiva, and the Moon.<- ॥ 3 ॥

<margin>red bracket</margin> First, to determine the auspiciousness of the five limbs of the calendar <term>Panchang: the traditional Hindu almanac</term>, the lords of the lunar days <term>Tithis: lunar days</term> are stated: The lord of the 1st day <note>Pratipada</note> is Fire <note>Agni</note>; of the 2nd, Brahma; 3rd, Parvati <note>Gauri</note>; 4th, Ganesha; 5th, the Serpent <note>Sarpas/Nagas</note>; 6th, Kartikeya <note>Guha</note>; 7th, the Sun; 8th, Shiva; 9th, Durga; 10th, Yama; 11th, the All-gods <note>Vishvedevas</note>; 12th, Vishnu <note>Hari</note>; 13th, Cupid <note>Kamadeva</note>; 14th, Shiva; and the 15th (Full/New Moon), the Moon. The purpose of stating these is that the deity who presides over a lunar day should be worshipped on that day; furthermore, they grant results for tasks according to their specific qualities and actions. As stated in texts like the *Ratnamala* regarding the purpose of lunar days: on the 1st day, one should not perform auspicious acts like marriage, travel, tonsure, consecration, or entering a new home; however, this specifically refers to the 1st day of the bright fortnight <note>Shukla Paksha</note>. In the dark fortnight <note>Krishna Paksha</note>, some of these actions are permitted, which will be clarified later. On the 2nd day: works related to the state, initiation ceremonies, consecrations, marriages, travel, and wearing ornaments are auspicious. On the 3rd day: the aforementioned works of the 2nd day, as well as travel-related deeds, crafts, tonsure, first feedings, and entering a new home are auspicious. On the "Empty" days <note>Rikta Tithis</note>—the 4th, 9th, and 14th—fire rituals, destructive acts, and imprisonment—

# Commentary with Language Translation - Chapter 1

Actions involving weapons, poison, fire, or combat are considered auspicious <note>In this context, "auspicious" means the timing is effective for these specific harsh activities</note>, while general celebratory actions are inauspicious on these days. On the **fifth lunar day**, all auspicious deeds achieve success; however, one must never give a loan (debt), as money given on this day is lost. On the **sixth**, all celebratory and nourishing acts are auspicious—except for oil massage, travel, ancestral rites, and using tooth-sticks <note>twigs used for dental hygiene</note>. Crafts related to war, architecture, jewelry, and clothing are also favorable. On the **seventh**, those deeds mentioned for the 2nd, 3rd, and 5th days are successful. <insert>On the **eighth**, deeds useful for battle, architectural work, crafts, royal duties,</insert> writing, matters concerning women, gems, and jewelry are auspicious. On the **tenth**, the deeds mentioned for the 2nd, 3rd, and 5th are successful. <insert>On the **eleventh**, all religious acts such as fasting and vows,</insert> festivals for the deities, architecture, military actions, and crafts are auspicious. <insert>On the **twelfth**, all religious and nourishing deeds for both stationary and moving beings are successful.</insert> <insert>On the **thirteenth**, the aforementioned deeds of the 2nd, 3rd, and 5th are auspicious.</insert> <insert>On the **full moon (Purnima)**, sacrificial rituals, nourishing acts,</insert> celebrations, military preparations, architecture, marriage, crafts, and all jewelry-related matters are successful. On the **new moon (Amavasya)**, only ancestral rites (Pitru-karma) should be performed; some scriptures allow for "fierce" <term>Ugra-karma (fierce actions like exorcism or combat)</term> deeds, but other celebrations or nourishing festivals should not be conducted. || 3 ||

->( In the Upajati Meter )<-

> The lunar days are named Nanda, Bhadra, Jaya, Rikta, and Purna; they range from inauspicious to mediocre to excellent. 
> In the bright and dark fortnights, they are respectively excellent, middling, or low; they become "Siddha" (perfected) when falling on Friday, Wednesday, Tuesday, Saturday, and Thursday. || 4 ||

In the three cycles of lunar days, the five classifications—starting with Nanda—follow in order: for example, the 1st, 6th, and 11th are **Nanda**; the 2nd, 7th, and 12th are **Bhadra**; the 3rd, 8th, and 13th are **Jaya**; the 4th, 9th, and 14th are **Rikta**; and the 5th, 10th, and 15th are **Purna**. Their results correspond to their names <note>e.g., Nanda means "joyous," Rikta means "empty"</note>. Furthermore, <insert>in the bright fortnight (Shukla-paksha), the first third (1st to 5th day) is inauspicious because the moon is weak;</insert> <insert>the second third (5th to 10th) is middling, and the final third (10th to the full moon) is auspicious.</insert> Conversely, in the dark fortnight (Krishna-paksha), the first third (up to the 5th) is auspicious, the middle third (5th to 10th) is middling, and the final third (11th to the new moon) is "low" or unfavorable. The meaning of the fourth line of the verse is that <insert>Nanda (1, 6, 11) on a Friday,</insert> <insert>Bhadra (2, 7, 12) on a Wednesday,</insert> <insert>Jaya (3, 8, 13) on a Tuesday,</insert> <insert>Rikta (4, 9, 14) on a Saturday,</insert> <insert>and Purna (5, 10, 15) on a Thursday</insert> are "Siddha" (perfected) days. The purpose of this is that <insert>"A Siddha Tithi destroys all defects,"</insert> <note>original: "Siddha tithir-hanti samasta-doshan"</note> meaning it grants success by removing astrological flaws like "month-void" or "burned days." || 4 ||

<note>original: "Muhurtachintamani"</note>

### (Shalini Meter)
<note>The Shalini meter is a classical Sanskrit poetic structure consisting of eleven syllables per line, known for its forceful and steady rhythm.</note>

> The lunar days known as Nanda, Bhadra, Jaya, Rikta, and Purna are considered "burnt" or destructive when they coincide with Sundays and the other days of the week in a specific order. Likewise, the constellations Bharani, Chitra, Vishvadeva, Dhanishta, Uttara Phalguni, Jyeshtha, and Revati are considered "burnt" when paired with the Sun (Sunday) and subsequent days. || 5 ||

<margin>Red bracket marks this paragraph</margin>
Starting from Sunday, the aforementioned lunar days (Tithis) like Nanda and others become inauspicious (harmful) in a specific sequence. For example:
**On Sunday, Nanda (1st, 6th, 11th); on Monday, Bhadra (2nd, 7th, 12th);** on Tuesday, Nanda (1st, 6th, 11th); on Wednesday, Jaya (3rd, 8th, 13th); on Thursday, Rikta (4th, 9th, 14th); on Friday, Bhadra (2nd, 7th, 12th); and on Saturday, Purna (5th, 10th, 15th). The constellations (Nakshatras) follow a similar pattern. For instance, **Bharani on Sunday**, Chitra on Monday, Uttarashada on Tuesday, Dhanishta on Wednesday, Uttara Phalguni on Thursday, Jyeshtha on Friday, and Revati on Saturday are considered "burnt constellations" <term>Dagdhakshetra; literally 'burnt fields' or 'combust stars'</term>. These harmful lunar days and burnt constellations should be avoided for all auspicious activities. || 5 ||

# Chapter 1: Including the Language Commentary

-> Page 5 <-

> *(Anustubh Meter)* The sixth and subsequent lunar days counted in reverse from Saturday, and the first lunar day on Wednesday, and the seventh on Sunday, are considered base. These periods, known as the "Saw-Blade Alignment," should be avoided for travel. || 6 ||

Counting in reverse from Saturday and in direct order from the sixth lunar day, as well as the first lunar day falling on a Wednesday and the seventh on a Sunday, creates the <term>Krakacha Yoga</term> <note>literally "Saw-Blade Alignment," implying a cutting or destructive influence</note>, which is base and should be avoided for auspicious works. In almanacs, this is also written as <term>Varadagdha</term> <note>literally "Burned Day"</note>. An easy way to identify these is that whenever the sum of the lunar day and the weekday equals 13, that day is "Burned." For example: Saturday (7th day) + 6th lunar day = 13; Friday (6th day) + 7th lunar day = 13; Thursday (5th day) + 8th lunar day = 13; Wednesday (4th day) + 9th lunar day = 13; Tuesday (3rd day) + 10th lunar day = 13; Monday (2nd day) + 11th lunar day = 13; and Sunday (1st day) + 12th lunar day = 13. Additionally, Wednesday on the 1st lunar day and Sunday on the 7th are separately mentioned as inauspicious. Furthermore, one should not use wooden sticks from specific trees like Neem for <gloss>brushing teeth</gloss> on the 6th, 1st, or New Moon days; according to some teachers, this is also prohibited on the 9th lunar day and on Sundays. || 6 ||

---

> *(Indravamsha Meter)* On the sixth, eighth, fourteenth, and the waning moon, one should not consume oil, meat, or fruit, nor undergo shaving or sexual intercourse. One should not apply oil on the thirteenth, tenth, or second days, and bathing with Myrobalan fruit on the New Moon, seventh, or ninth is harmful. || 7 ||

<margin> [A red bracket marks this section as significant] </margin>
On the sixth day, one should not have an oil massage; on the eighth, one should not eat meat; on the fourteenth, one should not shave; and on the New Moon day, men should not engage in sexual intercourse. Some opinions hold that intercourse should be avoided on all festival days. The fourteenth, the eighth of the dark fortnight, the New Moon, the Full Moon, and the Sun’s entry into a new sign (Sankranti) are considered "festival" days. For these prohibitions, the lunar day is considered at the specific time of the act, not necessarily lasting from sunrise to sunrise. Furthermore, <term>Tailabhyanga</term> <gloss>application of oil/paste</gloss> should not be done on the thirteenth, tenth, or second lunar days. This rule regarding bathing for the sake of removing dirt applies to the three castes excluding Brahmins. Finally, one should not bathe with <insert>powdered</insert> Myrobalan (Amla) fruit on the New Moon, seventh, or ninth days, as doing so diminishes wealth and progeny. On other days, bathing with Myrobalan and sesame paste grants merit; even according to Medical Science (Ayurveda), this medicinal bath improves the complexion of the skin. || 7 ||

---

> *(Indravajra Meter)* Twelve, eleven, five, three, six, eight, and nine; four, six, seven, two, eight, nine, and seven; twelve, six, seven, eight, nine, ten, and eleven—these are the Burned, Poisonous, and Fire-like alignments. || 8 ||

The <term>Dagdha Yoga</term> <note>Burned Alignment</note> occurs on: Sunday/12th, Monday/11th, Tuesday/5th, Wednesday/3rd, Thursday/6th, Friday/8th, and Saturday/9th.
The <term>Visha Yoga</term> <note>Poisonous Alignment</note> occurs on: Sunday/4th, Monday/6th, Tuesday/7th, Wednesday/2nd, Thursday/8th, Friday/9th, and Saturday/7th.
The <term>Hutashana Yoga</term> <note>Fire-like Alignment</note> occurs on: Sunday/12th, Monday/6th, Tuesday/7th, Wednesday/8th, Thursday/9th, Friday/10th, and Saturday/11th.
These three alignments yield results consistent with their names and are forbidden for any auspicious work. || 8 ||

*(Upajati meter)* The lunar dates beginning with Sunday, and the constellations of Magha, Vishakha, Ardra, Mula, Krittika,
->Rohini, and Hasta, create the "Bell of Death" <note>original: Yamaghanta</note> yoga; these must be avoided for auspicious travel. || 9 ||<-

Magha on Sunday, Vishakha on Monday, Ardra on Tuesday, Mula on Wednesday, Krittika on Thursday, Rohini on Friday, and Hasta on Saturday create the <insert>Bell of Death <note>Yamaghanta-yoga</note></insert> combinations. These specific yogas—the Burnt <note>Dagdha</note>, the Forked <note>Vishakhya</note>, the Consuming Fire <note>Hutashana</note>, and the Bell of Death <note>Yamaghanta</note>—are forbidden for auspicious works. They are especially forbidden for travel. However, remedies for these are found in other texts: their consideration is primary only in the region between the Vindhya and Himalaya mountains and not in other lands. Furthermore, if a benefic planet is situated in the angles <note>Kendras: 1st, 4th, 7th, or 10th houses</note> or trines <note>Trikonas: 5th or 9th houses</note> from the Ascendant, their fault is nullified. Some opinions state that only the first 8 Ghatis <note>approximately 3 hours and 12 minutes</note> of the Bell of Death are forbidden. The sage Vashistha opines that these four yogas give inauspicious results during the day and not at night. || 9 ||

(7)
# Chapter 1 - Including the Vernacular Commentary

The 7th of the dark fortnight of Ashadha, the 4th of Phalguna, the 3rd of Jyeshtha, and the 13th of Magha, along with the 6th of the bright fortnight, are considered "void" <note>Sanskrit: Shunya</note>. These are also known as the "Burned Months" <note>Sanskrit: Masa-dagdha; days that are 'consumed' and rendered powerless for good works</note>. || 10 ||

->Similarly, the following are rejected even if the lunar mansion is otherwise auspicious: Ashlesha on the 12th, and Uttarashadha on the 1st. || 11 ||<-
->Anuradha on the 2nd, Magha on the 5th.<-
->The three Uttaras on the 3rd, and Rohini on the 11th. || 12 ||<-
->Swati and Chitra on the 13th, Hasta and Mula on the 7th.<-
->Krittika on the 9th, Purva Bhadrapada on the 8th, and Rohini on the 6th. || 13 ||<-

The text now describes the flaws arising from the relationship between the lunar date <term>Tithi: a lunar day</term> and the lunar mansion <term>Nakshatra: one of the 27 sectors of the sky the moon passes through</term>. One should avoid auspicious works on: the 12th day if the moon is in Ashlesha; the 1st day in Uttarashadha; the 2nd in Anuradha; the 3rd in any of the three "Uttara" mansions <note>Uttara Phalguni, Uttarashadha, or Uttara Bhadrapada</note>; the 11th in Rohini; the 13th in Swati or Chitra; the 7th in Hasta or Mula; the 9th in Krittika; the 8th in Purva Bhadrapada; and the 5th in Magha. || 11 || 12 || 13 ||

->( **Anushtup Meter** ) <note>A poetic meter consisting of four quarters of eight syllables each</note> Rohini and Ashwini; Chitra and Swati; Uttarashadha and Pushya; Purva Phalguni and Dhanishtha. || 14 ||<-
->Uttarashadha and Shravana; Shatabhisha and Revati; Purva Bhadrapada; Krittika and Magha; Chitra and Vishakha.<-
->Ardra, Ashwini, and Hasta; Shravana and Mula; Bharani and Jyeshtha.<-
->In the months starting from Chaitra, these are called "Void Stars," and they cause the destruction of wealth. || 15 ||<-

In the month of Chaitra: Rohini and Ashwini; in Vaishakha: Chitra and Swati; in Jyeshtha: Uttarashadha and Pushya; in Ashadha: Purva Phalguni and Dhanishtha; in Shravana: Uttarashadha and Shravana; in Bhadrapada: Shatabhisha and Revati; in Ashvina: Purva Bhadrapada; in Kartika: Krittika and Magha; in Margashirsha: Chitra and Vishakha; in Pausha: Ardra, Ashwini, and Hasta; in Magha: Shravana and Mula; and in Phalguna: Bharani and Jyeshtha are "Void Mansions." Performing auspicious deeds during these times leads to the loss of wealth and resources. || 14 || 15 ||

->( **Anushtup Meter** ) Aquarius, Pisces, Taurus, Gemini, Aries, Virgo, Scorpio, Libra,<-
->Sagittarius, Cancer, Capricorn, and Leo are the "Void Signs" starting from Chaitra. || 16 ||<-

Regarding "Void Zodiac Signs" <note>Sanskrit: Shunya Rashi</note>: in Chaitra, Aquarius; in Vaishakha, Pisces; in Jyeshtha, Taurus; in Ashadha, Gemini; in Shravana, Aries; in Bhadrapada, Virgo; in Ashvina, Scorpio; in Kartika, Libra; in Margashirsha, Sagittarius; in Pausha, Cancer; in Magha, Capricorn; and in Phalguna, Leo are the void signs. They produce the same negative results as mentioned previously. || 16 ||

# Muhurta Chintamani (The Jewel of Auspicious Timing)

### Table of Void (Shunya) Designations by Month

| Months | Chaitra | Vaishakha | Jyeshtha | Ashadha | Shravana | Bhadrapada | Ashvina | Kartika | Margashirsha | Pausha | Magha | Phalguna |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| **Void Dates (Tithis)** | 8 & 9 <note>Both fortnights</note> | 12 <note>Both fortnights</note> | Dark 14, Bright 13 | Dark 6, Bright 7 | 2 & 3 <note>Both fortnights</note> | 1 & 2 <note>Both fortnights</note> | 10 & 11 <note>Both fortnights</note> | Dark 7, Bright 14 | 8 <note>Both fortnights</note> | 4 & 5 <note>Both fortnights</note> | Dark 5, Bright 9 | Dark 4, Bright 3 |
| **Void Constellations (Nakshatras)** | Rohini, Ashwini | Chitra, Swati | Uttarashadha, Pushya | Purva Phalguni, Dhanishta | Uttarashadha, Shravana | Shatabhisha, Revati | Purva Bhadrapada | Krittika, Magha | Chitra, Vishakha | Ardra, Ashwini, Hasta | Shravana, Mula | Bharani, Jyeshtha |
| **Void Zodiac Signs (Rashis)** | Aquarius (11) | Pisces (12) | Taurus (2) | Gemini (3) | Aries (1) | Virgo (6) | Scorpio (8) | Libra (7) | Sagittarius (9) | Cancer (4) | Capricorn (10) | Leo (5) |

---

**In the Indravajra meter: On odd dates starting from the beginning of the fortnight, the following Ascendants (Lagnas) are void: Libra and Capricorn; Leo and Capricorn; Gemini and Virgo; Sagittarius and Cancer; Leo and Cancer; Sagittarius and Pisces. These void Ascendants are not desired. || 17 ||**

<insert>
(From the start of the fortnight): Starting from the first day (Pratipada), these Ascendants are void on odd-numbered dates. For example: on the 1st, Libra and Capricorn; on the 3rd, Capricorn and Leo; on the 5th, Gemini and Virgo; on the 7th, Sagittarius and Cancer; on the 9th, Leo and Cancer; on the 11th, Sagittarius and Pisces. These void Ascendants should be avoided in all auspicious works. || 17 ||
</insert>

**In the Anushtubh meter: Narada says—The dates designated as void for the month, as well as the void Ascendants—**
->**These are to be avoided in the Middle Country (Madhya-desha), but they are not considered defective in other regions. || 18 ||**<-
**The "lame," "blind," and "one-eyed" Ascendants <note>Technical terms for specific planetary alignments that weaken an auspicious time</note>, and the void zodiac signs of the months—**
->**These should be abandoned in the Gauda and Malwa regions; they are not condemned in other lands. || 19 ||**<-

<insert>
Regarding the void dates and other designations mentioned, the sage Narada provides a specific distinction: the void dates and void Ascendants are only prohibited in the Middle Country <note>The central region of ancient India, roughly the Ganges-Yamuna doab</note>; in other regions, they carry no fault. Furthermore, the "lame," "blind," and "one-eyed" Ascendants (which are described in the section on marriage) and the void zodiac signs of the month should be avoided in the Gauda <note>Eastern India/Bengal</note> and Malwa <note>Central India/Malwa plateau</note> regions, but are not considered blameworthy in other countries. || 18 || 19 ||
</insert>

**In the Anushtubh meter: One should avoid the Sun in the Hasta constellation on the 5th date;**
**Mars in the Ashwini constellation on the 7th date; and the Moon in the Mrigashira constellation on the 6th date for all works. || 20 ||**

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of astrological timings from the previous section, specifically focusing on the neutralization of auspicious yogas by specific lunar dates and the introduction of the twenty-eight 'Ananda' yogas.</meta>

->Language Commentary Included - Chapter 1<-

The *Amrita Siddhi Yogas* <note>Auspicious planetary alignments believed to grant success or "nectar"</note> formed by the combination of weekdays and constellations can become harmful when combined with certain lunar dates (*tithis*). For example, the combination of Sunday and the *Hasta* constellation is usually successful, but if it falls on the fifth day of the lunar fortnight (*Panchami*), it is unfavorable. Similarly, Tuesday with *Ashwini* on the seventh day (*Saptami*), or Monday with *Mrigashira* on the sixth day (*Shasthi*), are considered contradictory. ॥ 20 ॥

->(Verse) Wednesday with Anuradha on the eighth day, Friday with Revati on the tenth day, ॥<-
->Thursday with Pushya on the ninth day, and Saturday with Rohini on the eleventh day. ॥ 21 ॥<-

Wednesday with *Anuradha* on the eighth day, Friday with *Revati* on the tenth day, Thursday with *Pushya* on the ninth day, and Saturday with *Rohini* on the eleventh day are all unfavorable. If such combinations occur, one should avoid all auspicious activities. ॥ 21 ॥

->(Verse) For entering a new home, for travel, and for marriage respectively, ॥<-
->One must avoid Tuesday-Ashwini, Saturday-Rohini, and Thursday-Pushya. ॥ 22 ॥<-

Although the aforementioned *Amrita Siddhi Yogas* like Tuesday-*Ashwini* are generally mentioned for all tasks, one must specifically avoid Tuesday-*Ashwini* for entering a new home (*Griha Pravesha*), Saturday-*Rohini* for starting a journey, and Thursday-*Pushya* for marriage ceremonies. ॥ 22 ॥

(Shalini Meter) The names of the yogas are: Ananda, Kaladanda, Dhumra, Prajapati <note>original: "Dhata"</note>, Saumya, Dhvanksha, Dhvaja <note>original: "Ketu"</note>, Shrivatsa, Vajra, Mudgara, Chhatra, Maitra, Manasa, Padma, and Lumbaka. ॥ 23 ॥

(Upajati Meter) Utpata, Mrityu, Kana, Siddhi, Shubha, Amrita, Musala, Gada, Matanga, Rakshasa, Chara, Sthira, and Vardhamana. These yield results according to their names. ॥ 24 ॥

The names of the *Anandadi Yogas* are: 1. Ananda (Joy), 2. Kaladanda (Staff of Death), 3. Dhumra (Smoke/Grey), 4. Prajapati (Creator), 5. Saumya (Gentle), 6. Dhvanksha (Crow/Inauspicious), 7. Dhvaja (Banner), 8. Shrivatsa (Auspicious Mark), 9. Vajra (Thunderbolt), 10. Mudgara (Hammer), 11. Chhatra (Umbrella), 12. Maitra (Friendly), 13. Manasa (Mental), 14. Padma (Lotus), 15. Lumbaka (Pendulous/Falling), 16. Utpata (Calamity), 17. Mrityu (Death), 18. Kana (One-eyed/Defective), 19. Siddhi (Success), 20. Shubha (Auspicious), 21. Amrita (Nectar), 22. Musala (Club), 23. Gada (Mace), 24. Matanga (Elephant), 25. Rakshasa (Demon), 26. Chara (Movable), 27. Sthira (Fixed), and 28. Vardhamana (Increasing). These yogas are formed according to the combination of the constellation (*Nakshatra*) and the day of the week; they provide results that correspond exactly to their names. ॥ 23 ॥ 24 ॥

# Language Commentary - Chapter 1

<page-num>11</page-num>
<header>With Language Commentary - Chapter 1</header>

<margin>Red bracket in right margin</margin>
For Sunday, count from Mrigashira; for Monday, from Ashlesha; for Tuesday, from Hasta; for Wednesday, from Anuradha; for Thursday, from Uttarashadha; for Friday, from Shatabhisha; and for Saturday, count from the current day's <term>Nakshatra</term> <note>Lunar mansion or star cluster</note>. The resulting number determines which of the yogas is active. For example, if it is Sunday and the Nakshatra is Ashwini, it is the Ananda yoga; if Bharani, it is Kaladanda. Similarly, for Monday, if the Nakshatra is Hasta, counting from Mrigashira gives 9, which is the ninth yoga, Vajra. Others should be calculated in the same way. Here, <insert>Abhijit</insert> <note>The intercalary 28th Nakshatra</note> should also be counted, making 28 yogas in total. || 25 ||

> (Shalini meter) Dhvanksha, Vajra, Mudgara, Ishu, Nabhi—these are to be avoided; also the twentieth, Padma, Lumba, Gada... Dhumra, Kana, Mushala, and the two Bhuryayamas; Rakshasa, Mrityu, Utpata, and Kaladanda are all [inscribed here]. || 26 ||

<margin>Red bracket in right margin</margin>
Regarding the duration to be avoided for these malefic yogas in times of necessity: for Dhvanksha, Vajra, and Mudgara, avoid 5 <term>Ghatis</term> <note>A unit of time equal to 24 minutes</note>; for Padma and Lumbaka, 4 Ghatis; for Gada, 7; for Dhumra, 1; for Kana, 2; for Mushala, 2; and for Rakshasa, Mrityu, Utpata, and Kaladanda, the entire 60 Ghatis <note>the full 24-hour period</note> are forbidden. In other texts, it is written to avoid three Ghatis for the Chara-yoga. || 26 ||

> (Anushtup meter) Counting from the Sun's Nakshatra to the Moon's Nakshatra, if the sum is 4, 9, 6, 10, 13, or 20, then Ravi-yoga occurs, which destroys a multitude of defects. || 27 ||

Counting from the Nakshatra where the Sun is positioned to the Nakshatra where the Moon is positioned (the day's Nakshatra), if the count is 4, 9, 6, 10, 13, or 20, then <gloss>Ravi-yoga occurs; this is auspicious for all</gloss> undertakings and destroys the groups of defects mentioned previously. || 27 ||

> (Indravajra meter) On Sunday: Hasta, Mula, the three Uttaras, Pushya, Ashwini. On Monday: Shravana, Rohini, Mrigashira, Tishya, Anuradha. On Tuesday: Ashwini, Uttarabhadrapada, Krittika, Ashlesha. On Wednesday: Rohini, Anuradha, Hasta, Krittika, Mrigashira. || 28 ||
> (Upajati meter) On Thursday: Revati, Anuradha, Ashwini, Aditi, Pushya. On Friday: Revati, Anuradha, Ashwini, Aditi, Shravana. On Saturday: Shravana, Rohini, Swati. These are called Sarvarthasiddhi by the ancients. || 29 ||

Regarding Siddhi-yoga <note>Yoga of Success</note>: On Sunday, the auspicious stars are Hasta, Mula, the three Uttaras (Uttaraphalguni, Uttarashadha, Uttarabhadrapada), Pushya, and Ashwini. On Monday: Shravana, Rohini, Mrigashira, Tishya (Pushya), and Anuradha. On Tuesday: Ashwini, Uttarabhadrapada, Krittika, and Ashlesha. On Wednesday: Anuradha, Hasta, Kritt-

->( In the Shalini meter )<-

## From Vishakha, Purvashadha, Dhanishta, Revati, Rohini, Pushya, and Uttaraphalguni, counting four stars each—
## There occur the yoga-cycles of Calamity, Death, One-Eyed, and Success, starting from Sunday; their fruits are equal to their names. || 30 ||

On Sunday, starting from the Vishakha <term>Nakshatra</term> <note>lunar mansion</note>, four stars in sequence create the Utpata (Calamity), Mrityu (Death), Kana (One-Eyed/Defective), and Siddhi (Success) <term>Yogas</term> <note>astrological alignments</note>. For example: on Sunday, Vishakha is Calamity, Anuradha is Death, Jyeshtha is One-Eyed, and Mula is Success. Similarly, these four yogas occur on Monday starting from Purvashadha, on Tuesday from Dhanishta, on Wednesday from Revati, on Thursday from Rohini, on Friday from Pushya, and on Saturday from Uttaraphalguni. Their results are exactly as their names suggest. || 30 ||

# Chapter 1: Including the Vernacular Commentary

(Translation) Bad combinations arising from the lunar day and weekday, or from the lunar day and the mansion of the moon, or from the mansion and the weekday;
->In the lands of the Huns, Bengal, and the Khasas, these three types of combinations are to be avoided. || 31 ||<-

Regarding the avoidance of <term>Kuyogas</term> <note>malefic astrological combinations</note>, it is said that those arising from the lunar day (<term>Tithi</term>) and weekday (<term>Vara</term>)—such as <term>Krakacha</term> or <term>Varadagdha</term> <note>specific "burnt" or "saw-like" inauspicious days</note>—and those arising from the lunar day and the lunar mansion (<term>Nakshatra</term>)—such as "Anuradha on the second lunar day"—and those from the mansion and weekday—such as "Bharani, Chitra, Uttarashadha, Dhanishta, Shatabhisha, Jyeshta, and Revati are 'burnt' on a Sunday"—and those arising from all three (lunar day, weekday, and mansion)—such as "Avoid all works on a Sunday with the Hasta mansion on the fifth lunar day"—all these faults are only prohibited in the <term>Huna</term> country, <term>Vanga</term> (Bengal), and the <term>Khasha</term> country (the Northern Highlands/Uttarakhand). They are not forbidden in other regions. || 31 ||

(Shardulavikridita Meter) In all matters, avoid an ascendant or a ninth-part division joined by the Moon or a malefic planet; avoid the middle of the day and night by one <term>Ghati</term>;
->Avoid twenty <term>Palas</term> at the center; avoid three days prior to an eclipse.<-
->From the occurrence of a portent, avoid seven days; a day is spoiled by ill-omened portents.<-
->Avoid the mansion pierced by an eclipse for six months, and avoid the mansion where a portent occurred until a benefic planet enters it. || 32 ||<-

Regarding things to be avoided in all auspicious ceremonies: an ascendant (<term>Lagna</term>) or a ninth-part division (<term>Navamsha</term>) joined by the Moon or malefic planets—the Sun, Mars, Saturn, Rahu, or Ketu—should be abandoned for all works. Similarly, while the <term>Abhijit Muhurta</term> <note>a specific auspicious time-window</note> occurs for 1 <term>Ghati</term> <note>approx. 24 minutes</note> at the exact middle of midday and midnight, its precise center (a <term>Ghatitryamsha</term> or 20 <term>Palas</term>—10 before and 10 after the midpoint) must be avoided. Likewise, avoid three days before a solar or lunar eclipse. Furthermore, an <term>Utpata</term> <note>portent or calamity</note> is defined as something contrary to nature, and is of three types: (1) Celestial—the appearance of comets, abnormalities in planets or stars, meteors, thunderclaps, or halos around the sun/moon. (2) Atmospheric—mirages (cities of the Gandharvas), rainbows, etc. (3) Terrestrial—earthquakes, abnormalities in trees, animals, or the behavior of fire and water. For seven days following such a portent or an eclipse, no auspicious work should be done; the same applies to the sighting of a <term>Ketu</term> (comet). According to other opinions, the rule for eclipses is: 7 days for a total eclipse, 6 days for a three-quarter eclipse, 4 days for a half eclipse, 3 days for a quarter eclipse, and 1 day for an eclipse of 1, 2, or 3 <term>Anguls</term> <note>a finger-width measurement of the eclipse's magnitude</note>. <insert>Handwritten signature/mark in red ink</insert>

<summary>This page details specific inauspicious astrological combinations and their regional applicability, while also defining natural portents (celestial, atmospheric, and terrestrial) that require the suspension of auspicious activities.</summary>
<keywords>Kuyoga, Tithi, Nakshatra, Huna country, Bengal, Khasas, Abhijit Muhurta, Utpata, portents, eclipses, astrological timing</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of astrological timing (Muhurta), specifically focusing on inauspicious omens and planetary phenomena that restrict the performance of auspicious activities.</meta>

<page-num>14</page-num>
<header>The Jewel of Thought on Auspicious Moments</header>

# The Jewel of Thought on Auspicious Moments <note>Original: Muhūrtacintāmaṇi</note>

Only the day itself is forbidden (during auspicious portents). One day is forbidden when the following portents <note>Original: utpāta; refers to unusual natural phenomena</note> occur: lightning strikes, earthquakes, the sound of a thunderclap during twilight, halos around the sun or moon, dust storms without wind, a blood-red solar disc at sunrise or sunset, trees exuding liquor, oil, or gum, or bearing fruits and flowers out of season. Other signs include the excessive rutting of cattle and birds in spring, falling stars, meteors, sounds without fire, smoke lines in the wind, a rose-red sky at twilight, disturbances in the sky, rivers drying up outside of summer, the earth suddenly cracking open, aquatic creatures coming onto land, mountains appearing to fly, or sightings of celestial women, divine chariots, or ghost-cities <note>Original: gandharvanagara; a mirage or illusory city in the clouds</note>. 

Further forbidden signs include seeing stars other than Venus during the day, hearing songs or instruments in the mountains without humans present, cold winds, the dancing of deer and birds, sightings of nature spirits <note>Original: yakṣas</note> or demons, hearing human voices where no humans are, darkness swirling in the cardinal directions, unseasonable snowfall, the sky turning black, or the birth of unnatural offspring from women, cows, goats, mares, or deer. A lunar mansion <note>Original: nakṣatra</note> pierced by a malefic planet, or one in which a planetary war has occurred, or where a terrible portent has manifested, is forbidden for six months. || 32 ||

->(Indravajrā Meter) A lunar mansion is unfavorable for six, three, or one month(s) respectively, depending on whether the eclipse is total, half, or a quarter.<-
->If the eclipse occurs at sunset or sunrise, three days before or after are forbidden; if it is a partial eclipse, three days on both sides are forbidden. || 33 ||<-

Regarding the forbidden nature of a lunar mansion based on the extent of an eclipse: if a total eclipse occurs, the affected lunar mansion is forbidden for six months; for a half-eclipse, three months; and for a quarter-eclipse, one month. If the sun or moon sets while eclipsed, the three days prior are forbidden, while the preceding time is auspicious. If the sun or moon rises while eclipsed, the three days following are unfavorable, while the preceding time is auspicious. If it is a partial eclipse, three days both before and after are forbidden. In the case of a total eclipse, seven days are forbidden. || 33 ||

->(Vasantatilakā Meter) The birth star, birth month, and birth date, as well as the Vyatīpāta and Bhadrā yogas, the Vaidhṛti yoga, the New Moon, the anniversary of a parent's death,<-
->the Tithi-Kṣaya and Tithi-Vṛddhi, the diminished or intercalary months, the Kulika and Praharārdha periods, and the first three ghatikas of Viṣkambha and Vajra yogas must be avoided. || 34 ||<-

<margin>Important</margin>
In auspicious ceremonies, one's birth star, birth month, and birth date <note>Original: tithi</note> are forbidden. The duration of the birth month is thirty days from the birth date according to the lunar calendar. Among the astronomical yogas, Vyatīpāta and Vaidhṛti are forbidden for all works. Also forbidden are Bhadrā, the New Moon <note>Original: Amāvāsyā; the day of the ancestors</note>, the anniversary of a parent's death <note>Original: śrāddha-dina</note>, a "decayed" date <note>Original: kṣaya-tithi; a lunar day that is skipped in the calendar</note> where three dates touch one solar day, and an "increased" date <note>Original: vṛddhi-tithi</note> where one lunar date spans three solar days. Furthermore, avoid the "decayed" month <note>Original: kṣaya-māsa; where two solar transits occur between two new moons</note>, the intercalary month <note>Original: adhika-māsa; where no solar transit occurs between two new moons</note>, as well as the Kulika and Praharārdha periods...

<summary>This page details various natural and astronomical omens (utpāta) and specific calendar conditions, such as eclipses and birth-related dates, that render certain times unsuitable for auspicious activities. It provides specific durations for these prohibitions, ranging from a few days to six months.</summary>
<keywords>astrological omens, eclipses, lunar mansions, birth star, forbidden times, Muhurta, Hindu calendar, portents</keywords>

# Chapter 1: With Language Commentary

(As will be explained later) similarly, the <term>Mahapata</term> and <term>Mahavaidhriti</term> <note>These are specific astronomical alignments or "great imbalances" calculated through mathematics</note> (these yogas are known through calculation), and the first three <term>Ghatikas</term> <note>A Ghatika is a traditional unit of time equal to 24 minutes</note> of <term>Vishkambha Yoga</term> and <term>Vajra Yoga</term> should be avoided. Regarding the aforementioned faults, the mention of the lunar day (<term>Tithi</term>) is an illustration; other texts also provide remedies for the decrease or increase of lunar days and constellations (<term>Nakshatras</term>). For instance, if Jupiter is in the <term>Kendra</term> <note>The 1st, 4th, 7th, or 10th houses of a birth chart, considered pillars of strength</note>, the fault of a decreased day (<term>Avama</term>) is nullified, and if Mercury is in the Kendra, the fault of a day spanning three constellations (<term>Trisprisha</term>) is removed. || 34 ||

-> (Verse in Anushtup Meter) <-
> In the first half of Parigha, the first five of Shula, six of Ganda and Atiganda, and nine Ghatis of Vyaghata must be avoided in all auspicious works. || 35 ||

The first half of <term>Parigha Yoga</term>, the first five Ghatis of <term>Shula Yoga</term>, six Ghatis of <term>Ganda</term> and <term>Atiganda</term>, and the first nine Ghatis of <term>Vyaghata</term> are prohibited for all ceremonies. || 35 ||

-> (Verse in Anushtup Meter) <-
> On the 4th, 6th, 8th, 9th, 12th, and 14th lunar days, one should abandon 8, 6, 14, 25, 10, and 5 Ghatis respectively; the remaining time is auspicious. || 36 ||

The 4th, 6th, 8th, 9th, 12th, and 14th are known as <term>Paksharandhra Tithis</term> <note>Literally "holes in the lunar fortnight," considered energetically weak or unstable</note>. In case of necessity, one must avoid the first 8, 6, 14, 25, 10, and 5 Ghatis of these days respectively. For example, after avoiding the first 8 Ghatis of the 4th lunar day, the remaining portion is acceptable for auspicious deeds. || 36 ||

-> (Verse in Anushtup Meter) <-
> Kulika, Kalavela, Yamaghanta, and Kantaka are determined by counting from the current day to Saturn, Mercury, Jupiter, and Mars respectively, and doubling the number. || 37 ||

Counting from the current day of the week: double the number of the day on which Saturn falls to find the <term>Kulika</term> <term>Muhurta</term> <note>A Muhurta is a period of 48 minutes</note>. Double the number of the day on which Mercury falls to find the <term>Kalavela</term> Muhurta. Similarly, double the number of the day on which Jupiter falls to find the <term>Yamaghanta</term> Muhurta, and double the number for Mars to find the <term>Kantaka</term> Muhurta. 

**Example:** On a Sunday, Saturn is the 7th day from Sunday. Doubling this gives 14; therefore, the 14th Muhurta on Sunday is Kulika. Mercury is the 4th day from Sunday; doubling this gives 8, so the 8th Muhurta is Kalavela. Jupiter is the 5th day; doubling gives 10, so the 10th Muhurta is Yamaghanta. Mars is the 3rd day; doubling gives 6, so the 6th Muhurta is Kantaka. One should determine the Muhurtas for all weekdays in this manner. These Muhurtas last for 4 Ghatis (96 minutes) and are prohibited for auspicious works. However, some sages believe that only the latter half of these Muhurtas is forbidden, while the first half is not tainted. Furthermore, these faults do not apply at night. The <term>Ardhyama</term> <note>A specific three-hour period</note> is always to be abandoned, as will be explained later. || 37 ||

It provides mathematical formulas for identifying inauspicious moments that must be avoided during religious or social ceremonies.</summary>

### The Cycle of Kulika and Other Inauspicious Moments

| | Sun | Moon | Mars | Merc. | Jup. | Venus | Sat. |
|----------|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Kulika / Bad Moment | 14 | 12 | 10 | 8 | 6 | 4 | 2 |
| Kalavela | 8 | 6 | 4 | 2 | 14 | 12 | 10 |
| Yamaghanta | 10 | 8 | 6 | 4 | 2 | 14 | 12 |
| Kantaka | 6 | 4 | 2 | 14 | 12 | 10 | 8 |
| Ardhyama | 7 | 9 | 3 | 9 | 15 | 5 | 9 |

---

### The Half-Watch Cycle

| Day | Number | Watch | Lunar Day |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Sun | 4 | 12 | 1 |
| Moon | 7 | 24 | 28 |
| Mars | 2 | 4 | 8 |
| Merc. | 5 | 16 | 20 |
| Jup. | 8 | 28 | 22 |
| Venus | 5 | 8 | 12 |
| Sat. | 6 | 20 | 24 |

> <term>Shardulavikridita Meter</term> <note>A complex Sanskrit poetic meter of 19 syllables</note> On the day of the Sun, the measures are 8, 12, 10, 14; for the Moon, they are 4, 6, 8, 9, 12, 13, 14; for the son of the Earth (Mars), they are 4, 3, 10... <note>The verse continues listing the numerical positions of inauspicious moments for each planet</note> ...these are the divisions for the followers of Bhrigu (Venus) and others. || 38 ||

> <term>Vasantatilaka Meter</term> <note>A melodic Sanskrit meter of 14 syllables</note> For Saturn, the measures are 10, 12, 14, 1, 2, 8, 10, 11. These are the "Evil Moments" known as Kulika, Kantaka, Kalavela, Ardhyama, and Yamaghanta, occurring in their respective parts. || 39 ||

For the sake of easy understanding, the "Bad Moments" (Durmuhurtas) are explained: On Sunday, the 6th, 7th, 8th, 10th, and 14th moments; on Monday, the 4th, 6th, 8th, 9th, 12th, 13th, and 14th; on Tuesday, the 2nd, 4th, 3rd, 6th, and 10th; on Wednesday, the 2nd, 4th, 8th, 9th, 10th, and 14th; on Thursday, the 2nd, 6th, 12th, 14th, 15th, and 16th; on Friday, the 4th, 5th, 6th, 9th, 10th, and 12th; and on Saturday, the 1st, 2nd, 8th, 10th, and 11th. These moments are considered censurable or "Evil Moments" and are named <term>Kulika</term> <note>The neck of the bird of time</note>, <term>Kantaka</term> <note>The thorn</note>, <term>Kalavela</term> <note>The time-tide</note>, <term>Ardhyama</term> <note>Half-watch</note>, and <term>Yamaghanta</term> <note>The bell of Death</note> as they occur. 

For example: On Sunday, the 14th moment is a "Bad Moment"; likewise, the 6th is Kulika, the 7th is Kantaka, the 8th is Ardhyama as well as Kalavela, and the 10th is known as Yamaghanta. One should know the names for Monday and other days according to the stated numbers. A <term>Muhurta</term> <note>A standard unit of time</note> is usually 2 <term>Ghadikas</term> <note>48 minutes</note>, but because the length of the day increases or decreases, here one must take the 16th part of the actual daylight duration. Whatever the daylight duration is for that day, divide it by 16; the resulting value should be known as one "Moment" (Muhurta). || 38 || 39 ||

> <term>Anustubh Meter</term> <note>The standard 8-syllable epic meter</note> In the regions of the Vipasha (Beas), Iravati (Ravi), and Shatadru (Sutlej) rivers, and in Pushkara, the eight days preceding the Holi festival are not desired for auspicious ceremonies like marriage. || 40 ||

<summary>This page details the calculation of inauspicious time segments (Durmuhurtas) like Kulika and Yamaghanta for each day of the week, providing a mathematical method to adjust these periods based on the actual length of daylight. It also notes a regional prohibition against marriages during the eight days before the Holi festival in specific North Indian river valleys.</summary>

# Chapter 1 (Page 17)

In the regions along the banks of the Vipasha <note>original: "विपाशा" - the modern Beas River</note> and the Iravati <note>original: "इरावती" - the modern Ravi River</note> rivers in the Punjab, as well as on the banks of the Shatadru <note>original: "शतद्रु" - the modern Sutlej River</note> and in the Tripushkara region, auspicious activities such as marriages are not considered favorable from the eighth day of the bright half of the month of Phalguna <note>February/March</note> until the "Hutashani" <note>the ritual bonfire of Holika</note> full moon. These restrictions do not apply to other regions. || 40 ||

> (Verse) Even if combinations like Mrityu, Krakacha, or Dagdha are present, they become auspicious if the Moon's transit is favorable. Some say these are only bad for the first watch of the day, while others say they are only forbidden for travel. || 41 ||

Among the various astrological combinations, those known as Mrityu Yoga, Krakacha, Vara Dagdha <note>literally "burnt days"</note>, Visha Yoga <note>poisonous combination</note>, and Hutashan Yoga are considered inauspicious. However, if the Moon is positioned favorably according to the rules of lunar transit <note>Gochar</note>, these "bad" yogas abandon their negative effects and yield good results. Some teachers hold the opinion that the fault of these yogas vanishes after one *prahar* <note>a period of about 3 hours</note> has passed. Others suggest that these omens are only prohibited for starting a journey and do not affect other types of work. || 41 ||

> (Verse in Bhujangaprayata meter) If a favorable yoga occurs on the same day as an unfavorable one, the good cancels the bad and brings success. Others say a pure Ascendant destroys bad yogas; likewise, the effects of Vishti and others are acceptable after midday. || 42 ||

On a day when a negative combination like Mrityu or Krakacha occurs, if a Siddhi <note>or Amrit Siddhi, meaning "achievement"</note> Yoga also occurs, it destroys the fruit of the evil combination and ensures the success of the task. Other authorities believe that if the *Lagna Shuddhi* <note>the purity/strength of the rising sign at the time of the event</note> is strong, the faults of Mrityu, Krakacha, and Dagdha are destroyed. Furthermore, the faults of Bhadra <note>an inauspicious period</note> and Vyatipata last only until midday; they are not considered defects in the afternoon. This same rule applies to Tuesdays and the *Pratyari* <note>the 5th birth star</note> constellation. || 42 ||

> (Verse in Shalini meter) In the bright fortnight, Bhadra falls in the first half of the 8th and 15th days, and the second half of the 11th and 4th. In the dark fortnight, it falls in the second half of the 3rd and 10th, and the first half of the 7th and 14th. || 43 ||

In the bright half of the month <note>Shukla Paksha</note>, the period known as **Bhadra** occurs during the first half of the 8th day and the Full Moon, and during the second half of the 11th and 4th days. In the dark half of the month <note>Krishna Paksha</note>, Bhadra occurs during the second half of the 3rd and 10th days, and the first half of the 7th and 14th <note>Shambhu-tithi</note> days. This Bhadra is technically the *Vishti Karana* <note>one of the eleven astrological 'divisions' of a day</note>. According to the method of calculating the *Karanas*, this period falls within these specific portions of the dates. This is considered a major defect and must be avoided in all auspicious ceremonies. || 43 ||

<summary>This page details specific geographical and temporal restrictions for auspicious rituals, explaining how favorable lunar positions or a strong Ascendant (Lagna) can neutralize negative astrological omens. It specifically defines the timing of "Bhadra" (Vishti Karana), a period generally avoided for important life events.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of astrological timing (Muhurta), specifically focusing on the malefic period known as Bhadra (Vishti Karana), following the general definitions of lunar days and divisions.</meta>

# Muhurta Chintamani

-> (Shardulavikridita meter) Five, two, six, four, eight, three, six, and one are the watches (Prahars) starting from which five Ghatis constitute the mouth of Vishti. In the directions of the twice-born (Brahmin), Indra, the sun, and Rama, starting from two, five, and four... The final three Ghatis in the southern parts are auspicious, known as the tail. During the day, the Bhadra arising in the latter half of the lunar day is auspicious; at night, that of the first half is auspicious. || 44 || <-

**The divisions of the Mouth and Tail of Bhadra** are explained: The first five <term>Ghatis</term> <note>A Ghati is a traditional unit of time equal to 24 minutes</note> of specific <term>Prahars</term> <note>A Prahar is a three-hour watch; there are eight in a full day-night cycle</note> of the lunar days (Tithis) starting from the 4th constitute the "Mouth" of Bhadra. For example:
*   On the 4th lunar day: the 5th Prahar
*   On the 8th: the 2nd Prahar
*   On the 11th: the 7th Prahar
*   On the Full Moon: the 4th Prahar
*   On the 3rd: the 8th Prahar
*   On the 7th: the 3rd Prahar
*   On the 14th: the 1st Prahar
The first 5 Ghatis of these periods are the Mouth of Bhadra. This is extremely inauspicious <note>original: "ati doshada"</note>. 

Conversely, the final three (3) Ghatis of the following periods are designated as the "Tail" (Puccha): the 8th Prahar of the 4th day, 1st of the 8th, 6th of the 11th, 3rd of the Full Moon, 7th of the 3rd, 2nd of the 7th, 5th of the 10th, and 4th of the 14th. This Tail of Bhadra is not harmful; rather, it is acceptable for auspicious works. Here, the calculation of Prahars begins from the start of the lunar day; the total duration of the lunar day should be divided into 8 parts (Prahars). 

According to other texts, the anatomical divisions of Bhadra are: 5 Ghatis in the mouth, 1 in the throat, 11 in the heart, 4 in the navel, 6 in the waist, and 3 in the tail. Among these, only the 3 Ghatis of the tail are auspicious. **Shripatiacharya** <note>A famous 11th-century Indian astronomer and mathematician</note> relates that once, when the demons (Daityas) defeated the gods, Lord Shiva opened his third eye in anger. A spark of the fire of fury emerged and took the form of a donkey-faced goddess with three legs, a tail, seven hands, a lion-like neck, a slender belly, and riding a corpse <note>original: "pretavahini"</note> to destroy the demons. The gods then praised her, named her **Bhadra**, and gave her a place among the <term>Karanas</term> <note>Astrological divisions of a lunar day</note>. 

Regarding the avoidance of Bhadra in essential tasks: Bhadra occurring in the latter half of a lunar day is auspicious if it falls during the day, and Bhadra in the first half is auspicious if it falls at night. Other teachers also suggest that Bhadra, Tuesday, Vyatipata, Vaidhriti, and Mrityu Yoga do not cause harm after midday. || 44 ||

-> (Anustubh meter) When the Moon is in Aquarius, Pisces, or the two Cancers (Cancer/Leo), she is in the mortal world; in Aries, Taurus, Gemini, or Scorpio, she is in heaven; in Virgo, Sagittarius, Libra, or Capricorn, she is below. Bhadra yields her fruit only where she resides. || 45 || <-

**The Abode of Bhadra (Bhadravasa)** is explained: If the Moon is in Aquarius, Pisces, Cancer, or Leo, Bhadra resides in the **Mortal World** (Earth). If in Aries, Taurus, Gemini, or Scorpio, she resides in the **Heavenly World**. If in Virgo, Sagittarius, Libra, or Capricorn, she resides in the **Underworld** (Patala). Bhadra gives her (malefic) results only in the world where she is currently residing, and not in the other two worlds. This is also a method of "avoiding" the negative effects of Bhadra. || 45 ||

<summary>This page details the specific timing and anatomical divisions of the astrological period Bhadra, explaining which portions are malefic (the mouth) and which are acceptable (the tail). It also describes the mythological origin of Bhadra from Shiva's anger and identifies her location in the three worlds based on the zodiac sign of the Moon.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on chronological purity (Kala-shuddhi) for Vedic rituals, specifically listing activities to be avoided during inauspicious astrological periods. It follows the previous section's focus on planetary influences on human affairs.</meta>

# Chapter 1: Including the Vernacular Commentary

### (In the Shardulavikridita Meter)
The commencement or consecration of step-wells, gardens, ponds, wells, and houses; the beginning or conclusion of religious vows; the first entry of a bride into the home; the sixteen "Great Gifts" <note>Sanskrit: Mahadana; refers to significant ritual donations like gold or land</note>; the Soma sacrifice and the Ashtaka Shraddha; the rite of hair-cutting <note>Godana</note>; the first harvest festival; the setting up of water-stalls; the first annual renewal of Vedic study <note>Upakarma</note>; the Vedic vows; the ritual release of a bull <note>Nilodvaha</note>; and the delayed sacraments for children whose primary time has passed; the installation of deities; || 46 ||

Initiation into a mantra; the first shaving of a child's head; marriage; the first sight of a new deity or pilgrimage site; taking the vows of a renunciant <note>Sanyasa</note>; the formal adoption of the sacred fire; the first meeting with a king; royal consecration; the commencement of a journey; the four-month seasonal vows <note>Chaturmasya</note>; the homecoming of a student; ear-piercing; and trials by ordeal <note>Pariksha; historical judicial tests like the "heated gold piece" test</note>—all these should be abandoned when Jupiter or Venus are set <note>combust with the sun</note>, in their "infancy" or "old age" <note>periods immediately after rising or before setting</note>, and likewise during an intercalary month <note>Adhimasa/Malmasa; an extra month added to the lunar calendar</note> or a diminished month. || 47 ||

**The Commentary:**
Regarding the purity of time, it is said that: building a new step-well, garden, pond, well, or house; house-warming; starting or finishing vows; the sixteen Great Gifts such as the *Tula-purusha* <note>donating one's weight in gold</note>; the Soma sacrifice; the ancestral rites; the hair-cutting ceremony; the collection of sacrificial fires; setting up a water-booth; the first *Shravani* ceremony; the various Vedic and Upanishadic vows; the ritual release of a bull for merit (not the one performed on the eleventh day of mourning); and the birth-rites of children *if* their primary prescribed time has already passed; initiation; the first viewing of a new deity or holy place; the daily fire sacrifice; the four-month sacrifice; the graduation ceremony; ear-piercing; judicial ordeals (used in legal matters); the entry of a new bride; the consecration of an idol; the thread ceremony; marriage; taking Sanyasa; the first audience with a king; coronation; and travel—all these acts should not be performed when Jupiter or Venus are combust, in their "infancy" or "old age," nor during an intercalary month or a "lost" month. 

<margin><insert>Note: This section is marked as "long" or "extended" in the original manuscript.</insert></margin>

On this matter, other authoritative texts decide:
> "The rites of hair-parting <note>Simanta</note>, birth-rituals, and those up to the first feeding of solids <note>Annaprashana</note> do not suffer any defect from an intercalary month or the combustion of Jupiter and Venus. || 1 || Likewise, all those rites whose primary time has passed should be performed when the Sun is in its northern course <note>Uttarayan</note>, while Venus and Jupiter are visible, and on a day when the five limbs of the calendar <note>Panchang</note> are entirely auspicious. || 2 ||"

This means that for the sacraments of a child from the womb-rite and birth-rite up to the first feeding of grain, if they are performed at their *fixed* time (for example, the naming ceremony on the 11th or 12th day, or the first feeding in the 6th month), there is no fault caused by an intercalary month or the setting of the planets. However, if the primary time for these acts is missed for some reason, then that act must be performed during the Sun's northern transit, when Jupiter and Venus have risen, and when the day's astrological components are entirely pure. || 46 || 47 ||

### (In the Shalini Meter)
One should avoid these acts when the planets are set, or when Jupiter is in the sign of Leo or Capricorn; some say also when a planet is in retrograde or rapid motion. When Jupiter is in the same sign as the Sun, or during the dark fortnight of the month, they likewise forbid the wearing of new jewelry made of precious stones. || 48 ||

<summary>This page lists specific religious, social, and royal activities that are prohibited during inauspicious astrological periods, such as when Jupiter or Venus are invisible or during an extra lunar month. It clarifies that essential infant sacraments are exempt from these rules if performed exactly on their prescribed dates.</summary>
<keywords>Muhurta, Malmasa, Jupiter, Venus, Samskaras, Marriage, Consecration, Astrology, Intercalary Month, Uttarayan</keywords>
